第一篇 人与居住环境——中国传统民居聚落

Preface & Overview
1
1. 人与居住环境——中国传统民居聚落基本理论与实践的探索
中文

人与居住环境——中国传统民居聚落基本理论与实践的探索

单德启

（国家自然科学基金项目结题报告，1992.2）

"人与居住环境——中国传统民居聚落"本身就是一个多元复合的命题。它包含着人对其所面临环境的认识和实践；包含着自然和社会环境本身；包含着人的生存、生产、交往、发展以及审美对建筑的要求；包含着民族、地域特征以及由这些特征所形成的文脉、信仰、心理等种种精神因素。如果我们脱离了孕育、生长乡土民居的当地自然、地理和社会条件，脱离了创建、使用这类民居最广大乡民的生活和精神世界，则"传统民居"仅仅是一堆僵化的符号。环境—乡土民居—人，是从形式到内容最有机的统一体。

现代科学研究本身往往是一个巨大的综合。本世纪兴起的就是综合研究、系统工程，它尤其重视群集效应和相互作用，整体的结构和功能。从人与居住环境的命题来研究中国传统民居聚落，实际上是把民居还原到综合的事物之中，从传统到现代化，从作为生物的人到社会的人，从生态自然环境到文化环境，是广度和深度、微观和宏观、时间和空间的交织。

系统的、整体的综合性能必然大于它所有要素性能的总和，强调整体思维和综合功利也必然会使我们站在一个新的效益和效率的高度来发掘传统民居。本文比较侧重于广大乡村传统民居聚落，尤其是发展中的不发达地区，而尽可能做地区的比较；努力使科研为现实服务。本文还侧重在现状调查研究的基础上，追溯历史，面向未来。

【概念界定】

——"中国民居"，是指包括大陆和台湾省在内的中华本土现存的"民居"，城市和工矿地区集团住宅、工棚式住宅不在论述范围；对于旧城改造中所指的传统民居，如四合院、里弄住宅，在界定上不严格，如在发生演变中涉及，但在现状以及改造中因为已与城市密不可分，本文不予涉及。

——"中国传统民居聚落"中的"聚落"（settlement）或称"集落"，既是学术范畴的建筑集团，又兼有小农经济体制下基层社会机制概念，它包括村、寨、堡、镇等。因此，位于这些聚落里的一些带有公共、文化、宗教性质的建筑物，如祠堂、鼓楼、家庙以及诸如桥、亭、寨门、路廊、更楼、券门、井亭等等都包含在广义的"民居"或"乡土建筑"范围。

——"传统乡土民居"语义与前面相同，英语中"本土的"（vernacular）、"土著的"（indigenous）、"传统住宅"（traditional residence）、"没有建筑师的建筑"（architecture without architect）等都有相同或相似的含义，强调土生土长、代代相传：(1)位于广大乡村和集镇；(2)产权归乡民所有；(3)基本上不是建筑师设计，而由使用者和邻里乡亲自建的；(4)居住模式大体形成于农耕社会而承传至今的；(5)一般多用乡土地方建筑材料和地方传统营造工艺建造。

——"传统"（tradition）是指历代传承下来的具有本质性的模式、模型和准则的总和。本文在引用词义时还包含以下内容：(1)"传统"是流动于历史的动态过程，而不囿于已经凝结成型的某一阶段；(2)"传统"有着主客体的双向作用即相互影响；(3)"传统"具有不同存在形态，包括心理、信仰、道德、审美、思维方式以及风俗、礼制、行为方式等。

——其他要明确的概念有："文化"（culture）在本文内泛指，即"声明文物"的广义文化；"建筑"，多指房屋建筑，建造过程以"营建"、"建造"表达；"环境"，在不冠以"自然"、"社会"、"生态"等限定外，均泛指自然环境；"地方"，本文主要指较大范围的"地区"（area），并非"地点"、"场所"的具体"地方"。

【鸟瞰：群星璀璨的中国民居】

中华民族各地区、各民族的传统民居聚落和乡土建筑异彩纷呈、群星璀璨，已越来越为海内外各界人士交口赞誉。从一棚一穴到"九十九道门三十六天井"的深宅大院，从木干栏到土楼群；从门神春联到砖雕盆景，从祖灵中堂到图腾柱；中国民居从表象到内涵，从本体到外延，从单栋民居到村寨聚落，都是独树一帜、博大精深中国传统文化的重要组成部分。

限于这方面发掘、研究的历史条件，迄今为止中国民居的分类还众说纷纭：中国曾发行了一套几十枚的"中国民居"邮票，可惜基本上是按省级行政区划分，显然缺乏学术上的严格性。例如："云南民居"邮票图像上的竹楼，怎么能包容云南26个民族的民居？又如："江西民居"和"安徽民居"的邮票图形，其民居建筑有何类型上的差别？中国建筑工业出版社陆续出版了诸如《云南民居》、《浙江民居》、《广东民居》等等，也是按省"各自为政"。显然，以行政区域给民居分类是不确切的。

按中国各民族来划分民居类型同样不很确切，白族和纳西族是渊源非常相近的两个民族，但由于地域相近，却同样拥有"三坊一照壁"、"四合五天井"模式的院落式民居；同样是彝族，哀牢山区的彝族聚落是一色的"土掌房"，而地域稍远的昆明地区彝族却是"一颗印"民居。同一个汉族，从窑洞到吊脚楼到四合院都有；而同一种干栏穿斗架木楼，却为侗、苗、壮等好几个民族所广泛采用。

按单体建筑结构形式、按建筑材料、按聚落团组构成等等来为中国民居分类，也都是似是而非，不尽如人意。

以上这些划分，单从行政区域、地区经济和文化、民族学或民俗学、建筑学或村镇规划理论等各自角度来看，也许各有道理，然而都相对地将中国民居的分类简单化了。这从一个侧面反映了中国民居及其聚落"多元"的特性。至于以大一统的模式来生硬套用到各地民居中去，就更不合适了。

本文并没有就中国民居分类进行专题深入，但笔者认为以生活在民居中的人的空间活动模式和生活特征来分类比较恰当，这就是：院落式、楼居式、穴居式。虽然这种分类并不很理想，也难以包括所有各类民居且又有交叉，但却把当今现存中国大陆分布最广的民居加以概括。

这种分类至少有如下好处：

第一，它立足于居住者的生活，而民居最重要的是真实地表达和满足人的生活；

第二，它有利于现存民居的溯源，探求它在发展过程中的传承和演变；

第三，它能反映该民居所处的自然和生态环境，它也能反映地区和民族的某些社会特征（如家族构成和邻里交往）；

第四，它还能形象地表达民居空间特征；

第五，对于寻求传统民居的改造和更新提供了最主要的有利和不利的根据。

现存中国各地区各民族比较普遍的和有代表性的民居归纳分类如表1所示。

如同汉民族占中华各民族人口绝大多数一样，院落式民居是中国最普遍的一类民居，也是民居形态中材料使用和结构技术最先进、构成因素最丰富、"礼"的层次最复杂和装修装饰最多样的一种类型。从某种意义上讲，它是农耕社会里最先进的一种模式，也是封建社会形态物化自然环境较理想的一种模式。院落式民居最主要的特征是封闭而有院落，中轴对称而主次内外分明。

汉民族聚集的地区以及与汉文化交流密切的少数民族地区（如白、纳西族等），或少数民族中比较发达的部分地区（如壮、彝族等），或与汉民族混杂而居的少数民族（如满、回族等），院落式民居是普遍采用的民居类型。其分布主要在东北、华北、中原、山东半岛和华南的平原和沿海地区，少数在西南的盆地平原，如四川成都、云南昆明和大理等地以及台湾岛的平原地区等等。

必须指出，院落式民居还有很多变异形态，如由穴居生土民居发展成的云南"一颗印"，如与干栏楼居融合演变的徽州天井式民居，如特定的历史和地段条件下主要立足于对外防御的闽、粤土楼集团式民居，等等。

楼居式、穴居式民居有着极其鲜明的自然生态地域性，也是保存原生建筑特征最多的民居建筑模式，中国的西南亚热带区和西北黄土高原干旱区是这两种类型民居最集中的地域。干栏式穿斗架木楼是中国楼居式民居最典型的代表，它把楼居的空间形态和组合，依山就势的支撑、悬挑和错层，木构件卯榫技术推向了极高水平；它和各少数民族鲜明的个性文化相结合，体现着丰盈的物质文明和精神文明。

而穴居式民居则是人类在另一种不利的自然条件下谋求生存的居住模式，像楼居式一样，特殊的自然生态环境造就了它特殊的建筑空间、材料和色彩、结构方式乃至建筑符号。楼居式民居和穴居式民居也有很多变异情况，替代部分木材的瓦顶、砖护壁的川西吊脚楼乃至浙江水乡楼居、下沉式窑洞民居与窑洞和地面土筑房院落的结合等等，便是实例。

从中国民居的类型和分布来看，无论院落式、楼居式、穴居式还是其他类型，都大体形成、完善和衍变在农耕社会中，即使有的模式年代久远，也几经修葺重建，实物超过三四百年的（为明代民居）并不多见。

但各类民居的共同之处，却是它们与地区自然条件（地形地貌、气候雨量、水文状况、森林植被等）及其生态环境、农耕或其他生产方式、社会的基层结构、民族的心理和气质、宗教信仰和风俗习惯等从上层建筑到经济基础的各个层面都有着密切的联系；人与居住环境相互关系是双向影响并协调和谐的；民居建筑及其聚落结构组合既有机又随机，既无法又有法，既理性又似乎无理性：是人、自然与社会的多元复合，是各个相关因素的多层次相互影响，是自然和社会的系统工程。

【溯源：源远流长的中国民居】

考古学和人类学研究成果表明，"穴居"是人类初始的"民居"，也是中国传统民居最原始的一种类型，公元前八千多年前旧石器时代晚期，黄土高原的先民就掘土而居。

在史籍中，"穴居"是先住民住所的统称。《易经》、《墨子》、《礼记》都有记载，如《易经》称"上古穴居而野处，后世圣人易之以宫室"；如《礼记》记载说："昔者先王未有宫室，冬则居营窟"；又如《诗经·大雅》："古公亶父，陶复陶穴，未有家室"。古公亶父是周王祖，大概是商代的氏族领袖，仍穴居，一般人就可想而知了。中国文字中，与"穴"相连的文字表述中有关建筑的很多，如表述生活的"窟"、"空"、"窗"、"灶"；表现贮藏的"窑"、"包"、"容"、"窖"；表述生产活动的"窖"；表述墓葬的"穴"、"穸"；乃至表现更复杂生活状况及精神内容的如"穷"、"究"、"穿"、"窜"等等，可见穴居对先民的广泛影响。

早期穴居，大体可分两类：一类是天然洞穴，主要流行在旧石器时代，遍及全国，它对狩猎、采集为生产方式的先民还凑合，而当进入农耕以后，洞穴为生就出现了很多矛盾，一是天然洞穴离耕地过远，二是居住空间狭小，生产力的发展使人们在住处需要置放工具和劳动成果的空间，此时家庭生活也渐次复杂化，如祭祖、交往等。第二类则是大体在新石器时代进入农耕以后普遍形成的"穴居建筑"。

中国黄河流域地区是文明率先发达的地区，也是人口比较密集的地区，迄今为止从史料分析和现状分析，"穴居建筑"在这里开始了重大的转变和突破。

考古资料表明，已发现的最早的天然洞穴是距今近一百万年的陕西兰田锡水洞，位于河岸大理石山崖，洞口光线可射入，有清泉，可通风排烟，空间约500平方米，显然适宜住人；遗址发掘有灰层炭屑和大量旧石器。此外，还有著名的周口店北京猿人洞穴等等。

西安半坡仰韶文化遗迹最值得重视。其房屋多呈方形或圆形半地穴式，显然是脱离天然洞穴不久；内有灶坑，大空间则深穴，小空间则浅穴。它表明人类已由山地降到平原，从狩猎采集进步到垦植，新石器时代工具已发展到普遍采用磨制钻孔技术的阶段，因而能更好地加工和改造自然资源。半坡遗迹至少可以在原始民居的发生中说明以下意义：

1. 它完成了地下向地面的转变和过渡；

2. 它实现了"土"和"木"两大建筑材料结合的"综合效益"，各自发挥性能；

3. 它孕育了空间分割、划分、功能分工、建筑部件的发展（如门、阶、墙、

【启迪：多元复合的中国民居】

中国民居是中华各民族人民世世代代长期奋斗而创造的自然—社会—人相互关联的广泛而又复杂的系统工程。经过千百年的风雨沧桑，更替、演进、碰撞、融合，肯定又否定，分解又综合，博取又筛选，形成了至今仍熠熠生辉、颇具启迪的一笔中国传统文化遗产。它在历史上已形成了协调自然环境、社会结构与乡民生活的居住环境和民居模式，形成了融合乡土材料、传统技术与民间文化的建筑风貌和民居格调。

传统民居最基本的经验，在笔者看来是物化了自然，而又人化了物。古老的《宅经》中就把民居及其环境描述为一个人："宅以形势为骨体，以泉水为血脉，以土地为皮肉，以草木为毛发，以屋舍为衣服，以门户为衬带，若得如斯是俨雅，乃为上吉。"人有生命机制、物质驱体，还有情感品格，民居亦复如此。把中国民居作为一个有貌、有神、有生命的机制来研究，方能获得更多、更本质的启迪。

中国民居，包含着三个由表及里、由浅入深的层次，兹分述于后：

表象层次——聚落位置、团组形象、植被绿化、入口寨门、水系水体、聚落中心、细胞组合、道路由巷、调节小品、边界围墙、大门影壁、房屋空间、屋顶组合、宅院天井、材料色彩、结构构造、装饰符号、图腾禁忌、附属建筑。

结构层次——生态、天人合一、生态观、形态实相牛、形态观、虚实兼备、形态观、怡养康泰、怡态观。

精神层次——整体思维、方式、综合功利、价值观。

中国民居形态那么丰富而不繁杂，巧妙而不做作，关键在于创造它的广大乡民习于农耕，适应了大自然变化的规律，特别喜爱对比中的和谐，渐变中的韵律，有朝有暮，有春华有秋实，生生息息往复不已，因而形成一套从自然辩证法到艺术辩证法的审美情趣，其特征为：

1. 美在自然。大自然是美的，中国民居亲山亲水，充沛的阳光，深邃的阴影，明亮的天空，浓密的树林，建筑生长于其中。美在自然，还有一层更具启迪意义的是，人工创造的美，也美得自然，这就是民居的形象、色彩、质感、光影等等，几乎都是与功能、材料和结构浑然一体，如马头墙防火，门罩遮雨，屋脊压瓦堵缝，"鸡罩"木楼空间防潮湿，穿斗吊柱头为结构之需要，等等。民居形态构成因素和装饰一开始就依附于实用需要，这就注定了它的"原生"和"自然"，"有机"与"质朴"的个性，其"拙"之美，"生"之美，是任何矫揉之作都难以匹敌的。

2. 有机随机。无法有法。各类民居形态构成中最主要的是建筑材料，乡土民居就地取材，山之木、原之土、滩之石、田之草等等，这就使得幢幢民居与自然环境有机于一体，宛如生长于大地。依山就势，就是一种随机，该悬挑则悬挑，该支撑则支撑，干栏木楼这方面表达得最为充分。在聚落的团组形态上，沿河溪则顺河道，傍山丘则依山势，有平地则聚之，无平地则散之，这似乎无法，但无法之中则寓有"顺其自然，因地制宜"之大法。

3. 耐看。这非常适合居住环境的形态要求。一个地区的民居模式，其大体相同的材料、结构和空间、平面的构成，形成了

【传统民居的保护与发展】

所有这一切不能不对现存的传统乡土民居聚落环境和居住模式提出挑战，引起了从中央到基层的政府部门、乡民大众的高度关心和重视。中国建筑师，尤其是从事传统民居聚落探索研究的学术界，理应面对现实，正视矛盾，寻求合理的演变发展途径。

中国传统民居聚落是传统建筑文化的历史遗存，又是当今众多特别是农民和少数民族地区人民的居住现实。这是传统民居与宫殿、寺观、园林建筑、祭坛等传统建筑类型最重要的区别。"传统民居"与"民居传统"是既有联系又有区别的两回事：前者是指仍然保存下来的有形的建筑实体，至今还作为乡民住宅在使用着；后者则主要是以观念形态存在着的一种无形的精神世界，例如：传承下来的历史上对人与居住环境的认识和理解，对建筑空间、居住聚落环境沿袭下来的习惯要求与审美情趣，它既见于以文字记载的家谱、志铭或史籍，又存于代代相传没有文字的风俗和经验之中。

传统民居作为一种有形的建筑实体，如前所说是一种历史文化的遗存，从文化的层面上看，我们应当保护、尊重，而从居住现状的层面上看，我们理所当然地要加以改造和发展。因为现存传统民居聚落的环境和居住模式，毕竟是数百年前在农耕社会所形成和完善的，与当今人的生活产生了错位。对现存传统民居聚落，作为一种文化的保存，显然只能是少量的、个别的；而作为现实居住要求对其改造，必然是大量的和普遍的。我们既不能忽视传统民居的历史文化意义而一概加以拆除改建，我们更不能无视那么多人的生活需要和国家发展需要而一概加以保护——事实上谁也无回天之力。

民居传统作为一种观念形态，体现着中华民族宝贵的整体思维和综合功利的价值观（生态、形态和情态的有机统一；人、建筑、自然和社会环境的有机统一；材料与技术、功能、审美情趣的有机统一），是一笔精神财富。其中少部分封建糟粕如风水说中的迷信部分、门阀等级观念、不科学不卫生部分应予剔除，大量的主导部分尤其是深层次的思想，还要加以发掘、整理和研究，作为传统乡土民居的改造和创造现代新民居的参考，乃至作为国家技术政策和管理机制的借鉴。

世界上许多国家都注意保存和保护传统民居文化。如，罗马尼亚在首都布加勒斯特建立"乡村博物馆"，有40种各地传统民居；土耳其在黑海西部的萨夫朗博卢镇就建立国家级文物保护区，这里集中着完整的奥斯曼帝国时期的传统民居，最古老的有300年的历史；韩国汉城以南的水原，按200年前传统民居式样建立民俗村。日本是非常重视传统文化的现代化国家，据东京大学渡边定夫教授介绍，全日本3300多个町镇，530个划为历史地区（其中80%还建立了

【探索：走向现代的中国民居】

1991年4月，《北京日报》报道我国第一个获得联合国环境署命名"生态农业村"的大兴县留民营村又大搞"八五"生态农业立体绿化工程，将使林木覆盖率农田达16.9%、村庄达30%。这个村就"如何充分合理地利用自然资源，持续、稳定发展农业生产，同时又保护和改善农村生态平衡"，对生产结构、能源体系进行调整，奋斗了十年，收到极大效益。总产值由1982年59万元上升到1990年的1700万元，人均收入由250元增加到1200元，还不包括公共福利。

1975年全村规划兴建170余户带有沼气池的一层独院平房，初步改善了居住环境，迄今又有110户就地改建了每户170～200平方米的二层楼房，全村集中兴办沼气，虽然还没有室内浴厕，但都预留了面积和空间……

的文化内涵往往都是与其实际需要的物质构成共生的，人们今天追寻古老的、质朴的乡土建筑文化的价值，恰恰是在追求这种"共生"；试想，如果脱离了"实际需要"和"自然物理"，着意于"形式"、"流派"之类的文化，乃至为文化而文化，那么传统民居也只不过是一幅立体的图画，失去了"建筑"的本质。所以说新民居如果说仍然是现代的"传统建筑"，没有这种文化与物质技艺的共生特征，也就失去了光彩和价值。"俗"并不等于"俗不可耐"，"俗不可耐"是有的，恰恰是丢掉这种"共生"而勉强往民居面孔上贴金抹彩，或者人为地耍弄建筑师的职业技巧，搞出了"土财主"式的成"假洋鬼子"式的东西。

其二是"其结构之系统及形制之派别，乃其材料环境所形成"，这就更有说服力地表明再生后的新民居不可能必然导致乡土传统和地方特征的丧失。各地区各民族的自然环境和社会文化环境是千差万别的，新民居如果仍然不失传统之真谛，即生态、形态和情态的统一，那也必然会千姿百态。即使是广泛普及某种更新了的建筑材料如水泥，也不会必然导致千人一面；"秦砖汉瓦"以后普及了黏土砖，仍然还有青砖、红砖、空斗砖白粉墙、磨砖对缝、镶边砖块石墙等五花八门的形制，闽东南传统民居中红砖墙体不少新花样，被誉为"红砖文化"。

再打一个比方，化纤织物代替了大量的原棉棉布，现代服装并没有大一统，反而款式、花色更多，新潮层出不穷。新民居普及了水泥制品，怎么可能就失去了地区的、民族的、传统的、乡土的风采了呢？老祖宗们彼时彼条件和手段有限得很，就已创造出群星灿烂、异彩纷呈的民居，我们和后代，难道就不能继续创造了吗？

在传统乡土建筑改造和中国民居再生的时代要求面前，任何消极悲观、无所作为都是没有根据、站不住脚的。

English

Human and Living Environment — Exploration of Basic Theory and Practice of Traditional Chinese dwelling Settlements

By Shan Deqi

(Conclusion Report of National Natural Science Foundation Project, 1992.2)

"Human and living environment — traditional Chinese dwelling settlements" is itself a multiple and composite proposition.

It encompasses human cognition and practice regarding the environment they face; it encompasses the natural and social environment itself; it encompasses human requirements for survival, production, interaction, development, and aesthetics regarding architecture; it encompasses ethnic and regional characteristics and the various spiritual factors such as cultural lineage, beliefs, and psychology formed by these characteristics.

If we detach from the local natural, geographical, and social conditions that nurtured and grew vernacular dwellings, and detach from the life and spiritual world of the vast rural people who created and used such dwellings, then "traditional dwellings" are merely a pile of rigid symbols.

Environment — vernacular dwelling — human being, is the most organic unity from form to content.

Modern scientific research itself is often a grand synthesis. What emerged in this century is comprehensive research and systems engineering, which especially emphasizes collective effects and interactions, overall structure and function.

Studying traditional Chinese dwelling settlements from the proposition of human and living environment actually restores dwellings to a comprehensive entity — from tradition to modernization, from human as biological being to human as social being, from ecological natural environment to cultural environment — it is an interweaving of breadth and depth, microcosm and macrocosm, time and space.

Systematic and overall comprehensive performance is necessarily greater than the sum of all its elements' performance. Emphasizing holistic thinking and comprehensive utility will inevitably enable us to explore traditional dwellings from a new height of benefit and efficiency.

This article focuses on the vast rural traditional dwelling settlements, especially in developing underdeveloped regions, while making regional comparisons as much as possible; striving to make scientific research serve reality. This article also focuses on tracing history and facing the future based on field investigation and research.

[Concept Definition]

— "Chinese dwellings" refers to existing "dwellings" in Chinese territory including mainland and Taiwan Province. Urban and industrial-mining area collective housing and shed-style housing are not within the scope of discussion.

For traditional dwellings referred to in old city renovation, such as courtyard houses and lane housing, the definition is not strict; if involved in evolution, but in current status and renovation because they are inseparable from the city, this article does not cover them.

— "Settlement" in "traditional Chinese dwelling settlement" (settlement) or "collective settlement" is both an architectural group in the academic category and also has the concept of grassroots social mechanism under the small-scale peasant economy system, including villages, stockaded villages, forts, and towns.

Therefore, some public, cultural, and religious buildings located in these settlements, such as ancestral halls, drum towers, family temples, as well as bridges, pavilions, village gates, road corridors, watchtowers, arch gates, well pavilions, etc., are all included in the broad sense of "dwellings" or "vernacular architecture."

— "Traditional vernacular dwellings" has the same meaning as above.

In English, "vernacular," "indigenous," "traditional residence," and "architecture without architect" all have the same or similar meanings, emphasizing being locally born and grown and passed down from generation to generation: (1) located in vast rural areas and market towns; (2) property owned by villagers; (3) basically not designed by architects, but built by users and neighbors; (4) living patterns generally formed in agricultural society and passed down to today; (5) generally built with local regional building materials and local traditional construction techniques.

— "Tradition" refers to the sum of essential patterns, models, and criteria passed down through generations.

In this article, the usage also includes the following: (1) "tradition" is a dynamic process flowing through history, not confined to a certain stage that has solidified; (2) "tradition" has a two-way interaction between subject and object, i.e., mutual influence; (3) "tradition" has different forms of existence, including psychology, belief, morality, aesthetics, thinking patterns, as well as customs, ritual systems, and behavior patterns.

— Other concepts to clarify: "culture" in this article broadly refers to the generalized culture of "civilization and artifacts"; "architecture" mostly refers to building construction, with the construction process expressed as "building" or "constructing"; "environment," without qualifiers such as "natural," "social," or "ecological," generally refers to the natural environment; "place" in this article mainly refers to a larger-scale "region" (area), not the specific "place" of "location" or "site."

[Bird's-eye View: Star-studded Chinese dwellings]

traditional dwelling settlements and vernacular architecture of various regions and ethnic groups of the Chinese nation are resplendent and star-studded, increasingly praised by people from all walks of life at home and abroad.

From a single shed or cave to the deep mansion with "ninety-nine gates and thirty-six courtyards"; from wooden stilt houses to earthen building complexes; from door-god spring couplets to brick-carved bonsai; from ancestral spirit central halls to totem poles — Chinese dwellings, from appearance to connotation, from本体 to extension, from single dwelling to village settlement, are all a unique and profound important component of traditional Chinese culture.

Due to historical limitations in excavation and research, the classification of Chinese dwellings remains controversial to date. China once issued a set of dozens of "Chinese dwellings" stamps, but unfortunately they were basically divided by provincial administrative regions, obviously lacking academic rigor.

For example: how can the bamboo stilt house in the "Yunnan dwellings" stamp image encompass the dwellings of 26 ethnic groups in Yunnan? Another example: what are the typological differences between the "Jiangxi dwellings" and "Anhui dwellings" stamp graphics?

China Architecture & Building Press has successively published books such as Yunnan dwellings, Zhejiang dwellings, Guangdong dwellings, etc., also "acting independently" by province. Obviously, classifying dwellings by administrative region is inaccurate.

Classifying dwelling types by Chinese ethnic groups is equally inaccurate. The Bai and Naxi are two ethnic groups with very close origins, but due to geographical proximity, they both possess courtyard-style dwellings with the "three compounds + one screen wall" and "four compounds + five patios" patterns.

Similarly for the Yi ethnic group: the Yi settlements in the Ailao Mountain region are uniformly "earthen palm houses," while the Yi in the slightly more distant Kunming region have "one seal" dwellings.

The same Han ethnic group has everything from cave dwellings to stilt houses to courtyard houses; while the same type of stilt-frame wooden building is widely adopted by several ethnic groups such as Dong, Miao, and Zhuang.

Classifying Chinese dwellings by single building structural form, by building materials, by settlement group composition, etc., are all plausible but not entirely satisfactory.

These classifications, from the perspectives of administrative region, regional economy and culture, ethnology or folklore, architecture or village-town planning theory, may each have their rationale, yet they all relatively simplify the classification of Chinese dwellings.

This reflects from one side the "pluralistic" character of Chinese dwellings and their settlements. As for mechanically applying a unified model to dwellings everywhere, it is even more inappropriate.

This article does not conduct in-depth special research on the classification of Chinese dwellings, but the author believes that classification based on the spatial activity patterns and living characteristics of people living in dwellings is more appropriate: courtyard style, stilt/tower style, and cave style.

Although this classification is not ideal and difficult to include all dwelling types with overlaps, it does summarize the most widely distributed existing dwellings in mainland China.

This classification has at least the following benefits:

First, it is based on the lives of inhabitants, and the most important thing about dwellings is to truly express and satisfy human life;

Second, it facilitates tracing the origins of existing dwellings and exploring their inheritance and evolution in the development process;

Third, it can reflect the natural and ecological environment where the dwelling is located, and can also reflect certain social characteristics of the region and ethnicity (such as family structure and neighborhood interaction);

Fourth, it can vividly express dwelling spatial characteristics;

Fifth, it provides the most important favorable and unfavorable basis for seeking the renovation and renewal of traditional dwellings.

The common and representative dwellings in various regions and ethnic groups of existing China are summarized and classified as shown in Table 1.

Just as the Han ethnic group accounts for the vast majority of China's population, courtyard-style dwellings are the most common type of dwelling in China, and also the type with the most advanced material use and structural technology, the richest constituent factors, the most complex "ritual" hierarchy, and the most diverse decoration and ornamentation among dwelling forms.

In a sense, it is the most advanced model in agricultural society and the ideal model for feudal society to materialize the natural environment. The main feature of courtyard-style dwellings is being enclosed with courtyards, with central axis symmetry and clear distinction between primary/secondary and interior/exterior.

courtyard-style dwellings are commonly adopted in Han ethnic gathering areas and ethnic minority areas with close cultural exchange with Han culture (such as Bai and Naxi), or relatively developed areas among ethnic minorities (such as Zhuang and Yi), or ethnic minorities living mixed with Han (such as Manchu and Hui).

Their distribution is mainly in the plains and coastal areas of Northeast, North China, Central Plains, Shandong Peninsula, and South China, with a few in basin plains in the southwest, such as Chengdu in Sichuan, Kunming and Dali in Yunnan, and plain areas of Taiwan Island.

It must be pointed out that courtyard-style dwellings also have many variant forms, such as the Yunnan "one seal" developed from cave-dwelling earthen dwellings, the Huizhou patio-style dwellings fused and evolved with stilt tower dwellings, and the Fujian-Guangdong earthen building complex-style dwellings mainly based on external defense under specific historical and site conditions, etc.

Stilt/tower-style and cave-style dwellings have extremely distinct natural ecological regionality, and are also the dwelling architectural models that preserve the most original architectural features.

China's southwest subtropical region and the arid region of the northwest Loess Plateau are the areas where these two types of dwellings are most concentrated. The stilt-style through-frame wooden building is the most typical representative of China's stilt/tower-style dwellings.

It pushes the spatial form and combination of tower dwellings, the support, cantilever and staggered floors following the terrain, and the wooden component mortise and tenon technology to an extremely high level; combined with the distinctive individual cultures of various ethnic minorities, it embodies abundant material and spiritual civilization.

Cave-style dwellings, on the other hand, are a living model for human beings to seek survival under another unfavorable natural condition. Like stilt-style dwellings, the special natural ecological environment has created their special architectural space, materials and colors, structural methods, and even architectural symbols.

Stilt-style and cave-style dwellings also have many variations, such as western Sichuan stilt houses with tile roofs and brick protective walls replacing some wood, and even Zhejiang water town tower dwellings, sunken cave dwellings and the combination of cave dwellings and ground earthen house courtyards, etc., are examples.

From the types and distribution of Chinese dwellings, whether courtyard, stilt/tower, cave, or other types, they were generally formed, perfected, and evolved in agricultural society. Even if some patterns are old, they have been repaired and rebuilt several times, and physical objects over 300-400 years old (Ming dynasty dwellings) are rare.

But the common point of all types of dwellings is that they have close connections with regional natural conditions (topography, climate rainfall, hydrology, forest vegetation, etc.) and their ecological environment, agricultural or other production methods, social grassroots structure, ethnic psychology and temperament, religious beliefs and customs from superstructure to economic base at all levels; the relationship between human and living environment is bidirectionally influential and coordinately harmonious; dwelling architecture and its settlement structure combination are both organic and random, both lawless and lawful, both rational and seemingly irrational: it is the pluralistic composition of human, nature, and society, the multi-level mutual influence of various related factors, and the systematic engineering of nature and society.

[Tracing Origins: The Long History of Chinese dwellings]

Archaeological and anthropological research results show that "cave dwelling" was humanity's initial "dwelling" and also the most primitive type of traditional Chinese dwelling. More than 8,000 years ago in the late Paleolithic era, the ancestors of the Loess Plateau dug caves to live in.

In historical records, "cave dwelling" is a general term for the dwellings of early inhabitants. The Book of Changes, Mozi, and Book of Rites all have records.

For example, the Book of Changes states "In ancient times, people lived in caves and wilderness; later sages replaced them with palaces and chambers"; the Book of Rites records: "In the past, the former kings had no palaces and chambers, in winter they lived in dug-out caves"; and the Book of Songs · Greater Odes: "The ancient ancestor Dan Fu, made pottery caves and dug caves, had no family chambers." The ancient ancestor Dan Fu was the ancestor of the Zhou kings, probably a clan leader of the Shang dynasty, still living in caves, so ordinary people can be imagined.

In Chinese characters, many characters related to architecture are connected with "cave": those expressing life such as "窟" (grotto), "空" (empty), "窗" (window), "灶" (stove); those expressing storage such as "窑" (kiln), "包" (wrap), "容" (contain), "窖" (cellar); those expressing production activities such as "窖"; those expressing burials such as "穴" (cave), "穸"; and even those expressing more complex living conditions and spiritual content such as "穷" (poor), "究" (investigate), "穿" (pierce), "窜" (flee), etc., showing the extensive influence of cave dwelling on ancestors.

Early cave dwellings can be roughly divided into two categories: one is natural caves, mainly prevalent in the Paleolithic era, throughout the country.

They were barely adequate for ancestors whose production methods were hunting and gathering, but when agriculture began, cave living presented many contradictions: first, natural caves were too far from farmland; second, living space was cramped.

The development of productive forces made people need space to place tools and labor成果 at home, and family life gradually became more complex, such as ancestor worship and social interaction. The second category is "cave dwelling architecture" generally formed after entering agriculture in the Neolithic era.

The Yellow River basin region in China was the first to develop civilization and also a relatively densely populated area. To date, from historical and current analysis, "cave dwelling architecture" began a major transformation and breakthrough here.

Archaeological data shows that the earliest natural cave discovered is the Lantian Xishui Cave in Shaanxi, nearly one million years old, located in a marble cliff by the river bank, with light entering the cave mouth, a clear spring, ventilation and smoke exhaust, space of about 500 square meters, obviously suitable for habitation; the site excavated ash layers, charcoal fragments, and大量 Paleolithic tools.

In addition, there is the famous Peking Man Cave at Zhoukoudian, etc.

The Banpo Yangshao cultural site in Xi'an is most noteworthy. Its houses were mostly square or circular semi-subterranean, obviously having just departed from natural caves; with fire pits inside, large spaces had deep pits, small spaces had shallow pits.

It shows that humans had moved from mountains to plains, progressed from hunting-gathering to cultivation, and Neolithic tools had developed to the stage of generally adopting polished and drilled technology, thus enabling better processing and transformation of natural resources.

The Banpo site can at least illustrate the following significance in the emergence of primitive dwellings:

1. It completed the transition from underground to above-ground;

2. It achieved the "comprehensive benefit" of combining the two major building materials of "earth" and "wood," each发挥 its performance;

3. It孕育 the development of spatial division, partition, functional differentiation, and building components (such as doors, steps, walls,

[Enlightenment: The Pluralistic composition of Chinese dwellings]

Chinese dwellings are an extensive and complex systematic engineering of nature-society-human interconnection, created through generations of long-term struggle by people of all ethnic groups in China.

Through thousands of years of vicissitudes — replacement, evolution, collision, fusion, affirmation and negation, decomposition and synthesis, absorption and selection — they have formed a precious traditional Chinese cultural heritage that still shines brightly and is quite enlightening.

Historically, they have formed a living environment and dwelling model that coordinates natural environment, social structure, and rural life, and an architectural style and dwelling character that integrates local materials, traditional technology, and folk culture.

The most basic experience of traditional dwellings, in the author's view, is materializing nature and humanizing matter.

The ancient Zhai Jing (House Classic) describes dwellings and their environment as a person: "A house takes its form and terrain as its body and bones, springs and water as its blood vessels, land as its flesh and skin, vegetation as its hair, buildings as its clothes, and gates and doors as its belts; if it is thus dignified and elegant, it is most auspicious." Humans have life mechanisms, physical bodies, and also emotional character; so do dwellings.

Only by studying Chinese dwellings as a mechanism with appearance, spirit, and life can we obtain more and more essential enlightenment.

Chinese dwellings contain three layers from exterior to interior, from shallow to deep, described as follows:

Appearance layer — settlement location, group image, vegetation greening, entrance village gate, water system and water body, settlement center, cell combination, roads and alleys,调节 small structures, boundary walls, gates and screen walls, house space, roof combination, courtyard and patio, material color, structure and construction, decorative symbols, totem taboos,附属 buildings.

Structural layer — ecology, unity of heaven and human, ecological view, form reality, form view, virtual-real complement, form view, nurturing and health, form view.

Spiritual layer — holistic thinking, method, comprehensive utility, values.

Chinese dwelling forms are so rich yet not cluttered, ingenious yet not artificial.

The key lies in the vast rural people who created them being accustomed to farming, adapting to the laws of nature's changes, especially loving harmony in contrast and rhythm in gradual change — with morning and evening, spring flowers and autumn fruits, the cycle of life endlessly repeating — thus forming a set of aesthetic tastes from natural dialectics to artistic dialectics, with the following characteristics:

1. Beauty in nature. Nature is beautiful. Chinese dwellings are close to mountains and waters, with abundant sunlight, deep shadows, bright skies, dense forests, and architecture growing within them.

Beauty in nature has an even more enlightening layer: the beauty of human creation is also naturally beautiful — this is that the image, color, texture, light and shadow of dwellings are almost all integrated with function, materials, and structure, such as horse-head walls for fire prevention, door canopies for rain shelter, roof ridges pressing tiles to seal gaps, "chicken coop" wooden tower space for moisture prevention, through-frame cantilever columns for structural needs, etc.

The constituent factors and decoration of dwelling forms were attached to practical needs from the beginning, which determines their "original" and "natural," "organic" and "plain" character. Their beauty of "rusticity" and "vitality" is unmatched by any artificial affectation.

2. Organic randomness. Lawless yet lawful. The most important factor in the composition of various dwelling forms is building materials. Vernacular dwellings use local materials — mountain wood, plain earth, beach stone, field grass, etc. — which makes every dwelling organically integrated with the natural environment, as if growing from the earth.

Following the terrain is a kind of randomness: cantilever where cantilever is needed, support where support is needed, and stilt wooden buildings express this most fully.

In the group form of settlements, along rivers they follow the watercourse, by hills they follow the mountain terrain, where there is flat land they gather, where there is no flat land they scatter — this seems lawless, but within lawlessness lies the great principle of "following nature and adapting to local conditions."

3. Enduring appeal. This is very suitable for the formal requirements of living environments. The generally similar materials, structures, spaces, and planar composition of dwelling patterns in a region form

[protection and Development of traditional dwellings]

All of this cannot but pose challenges to the existing traditional vernacular dwelling settlement environment and living patterns, arousing high concern and attention from central to grassroots government departments and the rural public.

Chinese architects, especially the academic community engaged in the exploration and research of traditional dwelling settlements, should face reality, confront contradictions, and seek reasonable evolutionary development paths.

Traditional Chinese dwelling settlements are historical relics of traditional architectural culture, and also the living reality of many people today, especially farmers and people in ethnic minority areas.

This is the most important difference between traditional dwellings and other traditional building types such as palaces, temples, gardens, and altars.

"traditional dwellings" and "dwelling tradition" are both connected and distinct: the former refers to tangible architectural entities that still survive and are still used as rural housing today; the latter mainly exists as an intangible spiritual world in ideological form, for example: the inherited historical understanding and cognition of human and living environment, the habitual requirements and aesthetic tastes for architectural space and settlement environment passed down, which are found both in written genealogies, inscriptions, or historical records, and in customs and experiences passed down from generation to generation without writing.

traditional dwellings as tangible architectural entities, as mentioned above, are a kind of historical and cultural relic. From the cultural level, we should protect and respect them; from the living status level, we should of course renovate and develop them.

Because the environment and living patterns of existing traditional dwelling settlements were, after all, formed and perfected in agricultural society hundreds of years ago, and have become misaligned with contemporary life.

For existing traditional dwelling settlements, preservation as culture can obviously only be for a small number and individual cases; while renovation as actual living requirements is necessarily large-scale and universal.

We cannot ignore the historical and cultural significance of traditional dwellings and demolish and rebuild them all; nor can we ignore so many people's living needs and national development needs and protect them all — in fact, no one has the power to turn back time.

dwelling tradition as an ideological form embodies the precious holistic thinking and comprehensive utilitarian values of the Chinese nation (the organic unity of ecology, form, and sentiment; the organic unity of human, architecture, nature, and social environment; the organic unity of materials and technology, function, and aesthetic taste), which is a spiritual treasure.

Among them, a small part of feudal dross such as superstitious parts in fengshui theories,门阀 hierarchy concepts, and unscientific and unhygienic parts should be eliminated; the large dominant part, especially deep-level ideas, should be excavated, organized, and researched, as reference for the renovation of traditional vernacular dwellings and the creation of modern new dwellings, and even as reference for national technical policies and management mechanisms.

Many countries around the world pay attention to preserving and protecting traditional dwelling culture.

For example, Romania established a "Village Museum" in Bucharest, with 40 types of traditional dwellings from various regions; Turkey established a national-level cultural relic protection zone in Safranbolu town in the western Black Sea region, where complete traditional dwellings from the Ottoman Empire period are concentrated, with the oldest being 300 years old; Suwon south of Seoul, South Korea, established a folk village according to traditional dwelling styles from 200 years ago.

Japan is a modernized country that attaches great importance to traditional culture. According to Professor Sadao Watanabe of the University of Tokyo, among Japan's more than 3,300 towns, 530 are designated as historical districts (80% of which also established

[Exploration: Chinese dwellings Marching Toward Modernity]

In April 1991, Beijing Daily reported that Liuminying Village in Daxing County, the first "ecological agricultural village" in China named by the United Nations Environment Programme, launched the "Eighth Five-Year plan" ecological agricultural three-dimensional greening project, which would bring forest coverage to 16.9% for farmland and 30% for villages.

This village, on "how to fully and rationally utilize natural resources, continuously and stably develop agricultural production while protecting and improving rural ecological balance," adjusted the production structure and energy system, struggled for ten years, and achieved great benefits.

The total output value rose from 590,000 yuan in 1982 to 17 million yuan in 1990, and per capita income increased from 250 yuan to 1,200 yuan, not including public welfare.

In 1975, the whole village planned and built more than 170 households of one-story independent courtyard houses with biogas digesters, initially improving the living environment; so far, another 110 households have locally renovated into two-story buildings of 170-200 square meters per household, with centralized biogas supply for the whole village.

Although there are no indoor bathrooms yet, areas and spaces have been reserved...

The cultural connotation is often symbiotic with the material composition it actually needs.

People today pursuing the value of ancient and plain vernacular architectural culture are precisely pursuing this "symbiosis"; just imagine, if detached from "actual needs" and "natural physics," deliberately focusing on "form," "school" and such culture, even culture for culture's sake, then traditional dwellings would merely be a three-dimensional picture, losing the essence of "architecture." Therefore, if new dwellings are still modern "traditional architecture," without this symbiotic characteristic of culture and material craftsmanship, they lose their luster and value.

"Vulgarity" does not equal "unbearably vulgar"; "unbearably vulgarity" exists, precisely because it loses this "symbiosis" and reluctantly applies gold and color to dwelling faces, or artificially manipulates architects' professional skills, creating "nouveau riche" or "fake foreign devil" things.

Second, "its structural system and form schools are formed by its material environment" — this more persuasively shows that regenerated new dwellings cannot inevitably lead to the loss of vernacular tradition and local characteristics. The natural and social-cultural environments of various regions and ethnic groups are vastly different.

If new dwellings still retain the true essence of tradition, i.e., the unity of ecology, form, and sentiment, they will inevitably be in myriad forms.

Even if a certain updated building material such as cement is widely popularized, it will not inevitably lead to uniformity; after "Qin bricks and Han tiles," clay bricks became popular, yet there were still blue bricks, red bricks, hollow-bucket brick white-plastered walls, ground-and-polished brick joints, bordered brick-stone walls and other diverse forms.

In traditional dwellings of southeast Fujian, red brick walls have many new patterns, known as "red brick culture." Another analogy: synthetic fiber fabrics replaced大量 raw cotton cloth, yet modern clothing has not become uniform; instead, there are more styles and colors, with new trends emerging endlessly.

If new dwellings popularize cement products, how could they lose regional, ethnic, traditional, and vernacular charm? Our ancestors, with very limited conditions and means at that time, already created star-studded and colorful dwellings. Can we and future generations not continue to create?

Faced with the era's requirements for the renovation of traditional vernacular architecture and the regeneration of Chinese dwellings, any pessimism and inaction are baseless and untenable.

2. 村溪·天井·马头墙——徽州民居笔记之一
中文

村溪·天井·马头墙——徽州民居笔记之一

单德启

（原载于《建筑史论文集》第6辑，1984.6）

"青山云外深，白屋烟中出；

双涧左右环，群木高下密。

曲径弯如弓，连墙若比栉；

自入桃源来，墟落此第一。"

山青水秀的徽州地区，大大小小的村落集镇，一派独特的民居风格，给人留下深刻的印象。在这里，人为的村镇布局和建筑空间，如曲径连墙等等，与大自然的山光水色融为一体，建筑充分显示了它的有机品格。所谓建筑的有机品格，我以为其内容包括：建筑群的规划布局，在功能和美学上与当地自然环境、生活习俗协调统一；建筑本身具有有机生长的特点，能满足使用灵活的要求；构成建筑形象的材料、结构构造、色彩装饰等具有地方特色。我国传统民居是探讨设计思想和借鉴处理手法的宝库；本文是学习徽州民居的一些记录和零星断想，拿出来求教于诸师友，以期达到抛砖引玉的目的。

【村溪——徽州民居的血脉】

传统民居集聚而成的徽州村镇，十有八九是和山光水色打成一片，或背山临水，或枕山面水，或依山跨水；直至"黄山向晚盈轩翠，黟水含春傍槛流"，"山禽拂席起，溪水入庭流"，窗外是山，槛外是水，山山水水都到家了！我们可以看到，溪水穿村而过的有：歙县的唐模，黟县的西递；溪水傍村而过的有：黟县的屏山，休宁的临溪；村落附近有较大溪水，小涧连于村落的有：黟县的碧山、

——穿村而过的溪水，两岸民居纷纷搭起路廊（即敞廊），有的还沿溪边置一溜飞来骑；连接两岸有圆木桥、板桥或石桥，还有的石桥上置桥亭；有的还在村中开凿人工池塘（如黟县宏村月沼，呈半圆形，图3）。这些廊、亭、桥、塘边，既可歇脚避雨，又可从事赶集贸易，夏季纳凉也是好地方，无疑可称为全村的"共享空间"（图4、图5）。

——村内街巷溪水傍流者，每户大门之外有石板条置其上，亦路亦桥，越水

县棠樾的七牌坊群，黟县碧山的塔，宏村的桥，太平县崔村的六角楼等等。

【天井——徽州民居的核心】

天井是徽州民居最具特色的空间元素。徽州民居的天井不同于北方四合院的庭院，它面积狭小，四周被房屋围合，形成一个垂直的空间。天井不仅是采光、通风的需要，更是徽州人"四水归堂"风水观念的体现——雨水从四面屋顶流入天井，象征"肥水不流外人田"。

徽州民居的天井具有有机生长的特点。建筑可以生长、加接，可以改变入口的方向，也可以像细胞分裂那样一分为二、一分为三，以适应变化着的需要。徽州民居这种有机品格，很说明天井是生长点。上面提到的石宅，就有大小7个天井。最早盖好的房子厅堂前入口和后院天井，为建筑左右生长而又维持家族联系提供了可能。而程宅一系列3个天井，分家后各自承担了独立的功能要求而不失空间的完整。直至今天，这种小天井还为我们充分利用旧民居提供了方便。解放后许多深宅大院分给多户居住，而各家皆有单门独院各得其所，不像北京或成都那种四合院，几家合居一院，每每相互干扰产生纠纷。

如同其他事物，建筑也是一个矛盾着的东西，它的内容（功能使用对空间的要求）和它的形式（平面和空间的构成），从建筑交付使用的那一天开始，就不断在矛盾着。民居尤为突出，因为它的构成要素紧凑而又经常不断变化——人口的繁殖、家庭组成的变化、经济和文化生活的发展等，无不要求调节使用空间。一般说来，建筑的这种矛盾统一取量变形式，即取改建、重新分割、增设等手段，以延续其生命。这就是建筑有机品格的标志之一。一旦矛盾非取质变不可，就要拆迁、更替，建筑生命即告结束，它有机生长的活力也到了极限。

传统的徽州民居一般都能保存百年甚至数百年。这些民居尽管是在社会发展缓慢的封建制后期延续下来的，但经历了这么长时期的使用，不断以量变的形式调节着需要与可能、内容与形式的矛盾，不断地分割、加接、改建和重新组合，而不需要彻底推倒重来，不能不认为这种民居具有很强的生命力。建筑的有机品格在我国地方建筑中是一个普遍的现象。今天，探讨它绝非想证明这些民居的

【马头墙——徽州民居的标志】

马头墙是徽州民居最具标志性的外部形象特征。马头墙又称封火墙，其最初的功能是防火——在密集的村落中，一旦某栋房屋失火，高大的马头墙可以阻止火势蔓延。随着时间的推移，马头墙逐渐演化为徽州民居的造型符号和审美对象。

马头墙的形式多样，有"坐吻式"、"印斗式"、"鹊尾式"等。其高低错落、层层叠叠的轮廓线，构成了徽州村落独特的天际线。白墙黑瓦与马头墙的组合，形成了徽州民居"粉墙黛瓦"的经典形象。

马头墙的设置与徽州民居的密集布局密切相关。徽州地区山多田少，村落用地紧张，房屋往往紧密相连，巷道狭窄。在这种情况下，马头墙既是防火的必要措施，也是在有限用地内争取空间高度的一种方式。同时，马头墙的造型变化也成为区分不同宅第、丰富村落景观的重要手段。

【新旧民居的比较与启示】

在调查中我们看到，解放后特别是近年来，徽州地区新建了不少民居。这些新旧民居比较起来，既是一脉相承，又在变化，是进行革新和探求一种新的出路的变化。它在消除旧的弊病而保存着有益和无害的东西。

从另一个角度看，大批旧民居仍然在解决着人们的居住需要。即使几家合住一幢，由于徽州民居有机品格的特征在一定程度上仍使很多住户比较满意，这种状况恐怕要继续一段相当时期。因而利用维修这些旧民居是必要的。当然，一些有学术研究价值的传统民居更应该列入文物保护范围。

此外，传统民居的规划设计特点，包括它的构思、布局、风格、处理手法的"词汇"等等，特别是这些总和体现出的建筑思想，如本文所提到的有机品格等应加以研究探讨，有分析地吸取到地方旅游、园林、纪念性建筑乃至新民居的规划和设计中去，逐步形成我国各地的带有地方特色的现代化建筑。歙县"古典园林建筑公司"设计施工的"多景园"和"新安碑园"两组园林文博建筑，都力图汲取徽州民居和宅园中的一些传统思路和处理手法。它们虽不无缺陷，但这种尝试和努力无疑是积极的。

"保存少量，利用大批，拆改发展，汲取经验"，这几句话能否概括旧民居的未来和未来的新民居呢？

月梁、粗壮的柱子了，银杏、梓木几乎不用。不少民居干脆硬山搁檩。木材的节约不能不认为是进步的标志。

——外墙在一层开了窗。人们打破了封建传统，不必顾忌泄了财气、招引匪盗，从而享受到更多的阳光和新鲜的空气。

——粉墙下加了勒脚。有的就地取材用块石，有的用一点水泥，改进了防水隔潮，显然体现了新的生产技术水平。

取消了门窗、隔扇等一些内装修上大量精细的木雕。门罩极大地简化了，砖雕部分大量以墨画代替，最简单的门罩是一块预制的钢筋混凝土构件。这说明主人支付能力有限，也说明人们的精神需要不再囿于自家的小天地。

还可以再列举一些。总之，新旧民居比较起来，既是一脉相承，又在变化，是进行革新和探求一种新的出路的变化。它在消除旧的弊病而保存着有益和无害的东西。

English

Village Stream · patio · horse-head wall — Notes on Huizhou dwellings I

By Shan Deqi

(Originally published in Architectural History Essays, Vol. 6, 1984.6)

"Green mountains deep beyond the clouds, white houses emerge from mist;

Two streams circle left and right, trees grow high and low.

Winding paths bend like bows, connected walls stand like comb teeth;

Since entering this Peach Blossom Spring, this settlement ranks first."

In the picturesque Huizhou region, villages and towns of all sizes display a unique dwelling style that leaves a deep impression. Here, the artificial village layout and architectural space — such as winding paths and connected walls — merge with the natural landscape of mountains and waters. Architecture fully demonstrates its organic character.

The organic character of architecture, in my view, includes: the planning and layout of building complexes coordinated with the local natural environment and living customs in function and aesthetics; the building itself having the characteristic of organic growth to meet flexible usage requirements; and the materials, structure, color, and decoration constituting the architectural image having local characteristics.

China's traditional dwellings are a treasure trove for exploring design ideas and learning from treatment methods. This article is some records and scattered thoughts from studying Huizhou dwellings, presented to seek guidance from teachers and friends, with the hope of throwing a brick to attract jade.

[Village Stream — The Blood Vessel of Huizhou dwellings]

Huizhou villages and towns formed by the aggregation of traditional dwellings are, nine times out of ten, integrated with the landscape — either backed by mountains and facing water, or枕 mountains and facing water, or straddling mountains and waters; even to the point of "Huangshan's evening green fills the windows, Yi water's spring flows by the railings," "mountain birds rise from the mat, stream water flows into the courtyard" — outside the window are mountains, beyond the railing is water, mountains and waters all come home!

We can see: streams passing through villages include Tangmo in Shexian County and Xidi in Yixian County; streams running alongside villages include Pingshan in Yixian County and Linxi in Xiuning County; villages near larger streams with small gullies connecting to the village include Bishan in Yixian County,

— For streams passing through the village, residents on both banks have built covered corridors (open corridors) one after another, and some have placed a row of "flying riders" along the stream bank; connecting the two banks are log bridges, plank bridges, or stone bridges, and some stone bridges have bridge pavilions; some have also dug artificial ponds in the village (such as the Moon Marsh in Hongcun, Yixian County, semicircular, Figure 3).

These corridors, pavilions, bridges, and pond sides serve as places to rest and shelter from rain, conduct market trade, and are also good places for summer cooling — undoubtedly the village's "shared space" (Figures 4, 5).

— For village streets and alleys with streams flowing alongside, stone slabs are placed outside each household's gate, serving as both road and bridge, crossing the water

the seven memorial archways at Tangyue in Shexian County, the pagoda at Bishan in Yixian County, the bridge at Hongcun, the six-cornered tower at Cuicun in Taiping County, etc.

[patio — The Core of Huizhou dwellings]

The patio (tianjing) is the most distinctive spatial element of Huizhou dwellings. Unlike the courtyards of northern Beijing siheyuan, the Huizhou patio is small in area, surrounded by houses on all four sides, forming a vertical space.

The patio is not only a need for lighting and ventilation but also an embodiment of the Huizhou people's fengshui concept of "four waters returning to the hall" — rainwater flows from the four-sided roof into the patio, symbolizing "fertile water does not flow to outsiders' fields."

The patio of Huizhou dwellings has the characteristic of organic growth. Buildings can grow, extend, change entrance direction, or divide like cell division into two or three parts to adapt to changing needs. This organic character of Huizhou dwellings well illustrates that the patio is a growth point.

The Shi residence mentioned above has seven patios of various sizes. The earliest built house had an entrance in front of the main hall and a backyard patio, providing the possibility for the building to grow left and right while maintaining family connections.

The Cheng residence's series of three patios, after family division, each承担 independent functional requirements without losing spatial integrity. Even today, these small patios still facilitate the full utilization of old dwellings.

After liberation, many large mansions were divided among multiple households, and each family has its own separate gate and courtyard, each getting what it needs — unlike the siheyuan in Beijing or Chengdu, where several families share one courtyard and often interfere with each other, causing disputes.

Like other things, architecture is also a contradictory entity. Its content (functional requirements for space) and its form (plan and spatial composition), from the day the building is delivered for use, are constantly in contradiction.

This is especially prominent for dwellings, because their constituent elements are compact and constantly changing — population growth, changes in family composition, development of economic and cultural life, etc., all require adjustment of usable space.

Generally speaking, this contradiction of architecture is resolved through quantitative changes, i.e., renovation, redivision, addition, etc., to延续 its life. This is one of the markers of the organic character of architecture.

Once the contradiction requires qualitative change, demolition and replacement become necessary, and the building's life ends, its organic growth vitality reaching its limit.

Traditional Huizhou dwellings generally can be preserved for a hundred or even several hundred years.

Although these dwellings were延续 from the late feudal period of slow social development, after such a long period of use, they have continuously adjusted the contradiction between need and possibility, content and form through quantitative changes — constantly dividing, extending, renovating, and recombining — without needing to be completely demolished and rebuilt.

This cannot but be regarded as demonstrating strong vitality. The organic character of architecture is a universal phenomenon in China's regional architecture. Today, exploring it is by no means to prove that these dwellings

[horse-head wall — The Symbol of Huizhou dwellings]

The horse-head wall (matouqiang) is the most iconic external image feature of Huizhou dwellings. Also called fire-sealing wall, its original function was fire prevention — in dense villages, once a house catches fire, the tall horse-head wall can prevent the fire from spreading. Over time, the horse-head wall gradually evolved into a造型 symbol and aesthetic object of Huizhou dwellings.

horse-head walls have various forms, including "sitting-kiss style," "seal-bucket style," and "magpie-tail style." Their高低错落, layered contour lines constitute the unique skyline of Huizhou villages. The combination of white walls, black tiles, and horse-head walls forms the classic image of Huizhou dwellings — "powder walls and dark tiles."

The setting of horse-head walls is closely related to the dense layout of Huizhou dwellings. The Huizhou region has many mountains and little farmland, with tight village land and houses often closely connected with narrow alleys.

In this situation, the horse-head wall is both a necessary fire prevention measure and a way to争取 spatial height within limited land. At the same time, the造型 variations of horse-head walls also become an important means of distinguishing different residences and enriching the village landscape.

[Comparison and Enlightenment of Old and New dwellings]

In our investigation, we saw that after liberation, especially in recent years, many new dwellings have been built in the Huizhou region. Comparing these old and new dwellings, they are both in the same lineage and changing — a change of innovation and exploration of a new path. It eliminates old drawbacks while preserving beneficial and harmless elements.

From another perspective, a large number of old dwellings still meet people's housing needs. Even when several families share one building, due to the organic character of Huizhou dwellings, many households are still relatively satisfied to a certain extent, and this situation may continue for quite some time.

Therefore, utilizing and maintaining these old dwellings is necessary. Of course, some traditional dwellings with academic research value should be listed as cultural relic protection scope.

In addition, the planning and design characteristics of traditional dwellings — including their conception, layout, style, the "vocabulary" of treatment methods, etc., and especially the architectural ideas embodied in their totality, such as the organic character mentioned in this article — should be studied and explored, and analytically absorbed into the planning and design of local tourism, gardens, commemorative buildings, and even new dwellings, gradually forming modern architecture with local characteristics in various parts of China.

The "Duojing Garden" and "Xin'an Stele Garden"两组 garden and cultural-relic buildings designed and constructed by the Shexian "Classical Garden Architecture Company" both strive to absorb some traditional ideas and treatment methods from Huizhou dwellings and residential gardens.

Although they are not without flaws, such attempts and efforts are undoubtedly positive.

"Preserve a small number, utilize the majority, demolish and renovate for development, absorb experience" — can these few words summarize the future of old dwellings and the new dwellings of the future?

Moon beams and thick columns are gone; ginkgo and catalpa wood are almost never used. Many dwellings simply use hard-gable rafters. The conservation of timber cannot but be regarded as a sign of progress.

— Windows are opened on the first floor of exterior walls. People have broken feudal traditions, no longer worrying about leaking wealth or attracting bandits, thus enjoying more sunlight and fresh air.

— Footings are added under plastered walls. Some use local stone, some use a bit of cement, improving waterproofing and moisture isolation, clearly reflecting new production technology levels.

Some elaborate wood carvings on interior decorations such as doors, windows, and lattice partitions have been eliminated. Door canopies have been greatly simplified; brick carving parts are largely replaced by ink paintings, and the simplest door canopy is a precast reinforced concrete component.

This indicates the owners' limited payment ability and also shows that people's spiritual needs are no longer confined to their own small world.

More examples could be listed. In summary, comparing old and new dwellings, they are both in the same lineage and changing — a change of innovation and exploration of a new path. It eliminates old drawbacks while preserving beneficial and harmless elements.

本篇术语 Key Terms

村溪 / Village Stream (cunxi) 天井 / Patio (tianjing) 马头墙 / Horse-head Wall (matouqiang) 徽州民居 / Huizhou Dwellings 四水归堂 / Four Waters Returning to Hall (fengshui concept) 有机品格 / Organic Character 粉墙黛瓦 / Powder Walls and Dark Tiles 宏村月沼 / Hongcun Moon Marsh 共享空间 / Shared Space 封火墙 / Fire-sealing Wall 唐模西递 / Tangmo / Xidi (Huizhou villages) 新安碑园多景园 / Xin'an Stele Garden / Duojing Garden
3
3. 黟县宏村规划探源——徽州民居笔记之二
中文

黟县宏村规划探源——徽州民居笔记之二

单德启

（原载于《建筑史论文集》第8辑，1987.5）

徽州地处安徽省南部群山之中，黟县则处群山之隘。"徽之为郡在山岭川谷崎岖之中"（顾炎武：《天下郡国利病书》卷32），山地丘陵占十分之九；新安江及其支流奔泻其间，山川交相辉映，雄伟秀丽兼而有之。徽州所属六县有两个奇特的县名，一为歙，一为黟，都是早在秦始皇时就置县的中华第一批县制，迄今已有两千多年历史了。现歙为大县（39万人口），黟为小县（仅9万多人），但是传统民居保存得最多、古村镇原型改变得最少的，在徽州要首推黟县了。本文是笔者结合工作多次到黟县宏村参观、调查和实测的学习笔记。

【一处令人叫绝的村落】

清代诗人胡成浚慕名游历宏村后，写下这样的诗句：

何事就此卜邻居，

花月南湖画不如；

浣汲未妨溪路远，

家家门巷有清泉。

宏村，又名泓村，南距黟县城二十华里，处于四围皆山的黟县盆地北缘。它背依海拔360米（相对高度60多米）的、拥有成片檫林的雷岗；南临新安江水支邕溪、羊栈河二水交汇处，所谓"枕高岗、面流水，一望无际"，地理位置十分优越。现有355户、1180余人，分属两个生产队。它是黟县著名的风景秀丽动人的古老村落之一，村内外有"雷岗点翠"、"南湖秋月"等八景。村口溪边原有睢阳亭，现亭已毁，旧址旁一株高18米、直径1.8米的红杨

【宏村水系规划的三个发展阶段】

宏村的规划最令人叫绝的是其水系。宏村的水系不是一次建成的，而是经历了三个发展阶段，逐步完善的。

第一阶段：南宋绍兴年间（1131-1162年），宏村始祖汪彦济因战乱从黟县奇墅村迁来，在雷岗山畔建宅十三间，是为宏村之始。此时的宏村只是一个依山而建的小村落，尚无系统的水系规划。

第二阶段：明永乐年间（1403-1424年），宏村汪氏七十六世祖汪思齐（曾任山西运粟主簿）邀请风水先生何可达（休宁人），"遍阅山川，详审脉络"，制订了宏村的水系规划。何可达认为宏村的形势是"牛形"——雷岗山为牛头，村口的两棵古树为牛角，民居为牛躯，月沼为牛胃，水圳为牛肠，南湖为牛肚。于是在村中心开挖月沼（半月形池塘），作为"牛胃"，并开凿水圳（人工水渠）四百余米，引西溪水入村，流经家家户户，作为"牛肠"。这是宏村水系规划的关键阶段，奠定了宏村"牛形村落"的基本格局。

第三阶段：明万历年间（1580年左右），宏村新的发展给居住环境又带来了新的矛盾。月沼、水圳兴建以来150多年了，人口继续衍殖，建筑更加密集，突出的矛盾仍然是水源。人们忧虑南山赤日烈焰，村内即使有月沼蓄潴之"内阳水"亦不敷需

明代是徽州继南宋之后第二次大发展的重要时期。此时资本主义萌发，善于经商致富的大批徽州人纷纷在故乡购田置屋；另一方面，程朱理学也发展到极盛时期，宗族势力兴建了大批祠堂，而地主富绅也借助宗法思想和势力大规模兼并土地。这是一个有需要、有力量进行一定范围内大规模建设的时期，宏村水系基本上形成了定局。这是发展的第二阶段。

到明万历年间（1580年左右），宏村新的发展给居住环境又带来了新的矛盾。月沼、水圳兴建以来150多年了，人口继续衍殖，建筑更加密集，突出的矛盾仍然是水源。人们忧虑南山赤日烈焰，村内即使有月沼蓄潴之"内阳水"亦不敷需要。于是汪奎元等辈"倡引西山之泉，凿深而大之"，在村南开挖南湖，作为"牛肚"。南湖呈弓形，面积约1.8公顷，湖岸用青石砌成，湖中筑有"画桥"将湖分为东西两部分。南湖的建成，使宏村的水系更加完善，形成了"山为牛头、树为角、屋为身、桥为脚、塘为胃、圳为肠、湖为肚"的完整牛形格局。

【宏村规划的启示】

宏村的规划，是历史的产物，当然不无缺点，而不应今天照搬照抄。但是，在强调重视村镇的发展和规划、把乡村建设提到日程上来的今天，宏村的格局、水系、建筑与环境空间等等本身显然有借鉴和参考价值。而笔者认为更重要的是：社会主义现代化新农村在规划和建设上，要是不把物质文明和精神文明结合起来，要是不注重"邀山川之效灵"，一味把地貌统统用推土机推得平平的，把河沟砌得直直的，盖上一排排雷同单一的兵营式，或是不讲规划，任其乱盖乱建，则连几百年前的老祖宗都不如了。宏村对我们的启示，在这一点上无疑比那些具体手法借鉴重要得多。

宏村的规划体现了中国传统村落规划的智慧：

1. 顺应自然，利用自然——宏村背山面水，利用地形地势，将自然水系引入村中，形成了人与自然的和谐统一。

2. 实用与审美相结合——月沼和南湖既是消防、饮用、洗涤的实用水源，又是村落的景观中心，具有很高的审美价值。

3. 系统规划，逐步完善——宏村的水系不是一次建成的，而是经过几代人的努力，逐步完善的，体现了规划的系统性和可持续性。

4. 文化内涵的注入——将村落形态与"牛形"风水观念结合，赋予村落以文化象征意义，增强了村民的认同感和凝聚力。

的结合是"在普通常识与活泼灵感之间，取得绝对的、本质上的和谐"。（引自《德拉克罗瓦日记》）

（笔者在调研、写作中曾得到黟县基建局周海华，宏村文化站汪良同志的帮助，谨此致谢）

English

Exploring the Origins of Hongcun Village planning in Yixian County — Notes on Huizhou dwellings II

By Shan Deqi

(Originally published in Architectural History Essays, Vol. 8, 1987.5)

Huizhou is located among the mountains of southern Anhui Province, and Yixian County is situated at a mountain pass. "Huizhou as a prefecture lies among rugged mountains and valleys" (Gu Yanwu: Treatise on the Advantages and Disadvantages of Counties and Prefectures Under Heaven, Vol.

32), with mountains and hills accounting for nine-tenths of the area; the Xin'an River and its tributaries rush through it, with mountains and rivers reflecting each other, combining grandeur and beauty.

Among the six counties under Huizhou, two have unusual county names: She and Yi, both among the first batch of counties established in China as early as the Qin Shi Huang era, with a history of over two thousand years.

Today She is a large county (390,000 population) and Yi is a small county (only over 90,000), but in terms of preserving the most traditional dwellings and having the least altered ancient village prototypes, Yixian County ranks first in Huizhou.

This article is the author's study notes from multiple visits, investigations, and surveys of Hongcun Village in Yixian County in connection with work.

[A Stunning Village]

After visiting Hongcun, Qing dynasty poet Hu Chengjun wrote the following poem:

Why choose this place as your neighbor?

The flower moon at South Lake surpasses paintings;

Washing and drawing water do not hinder the distant stream path,

Every household's gate and alley has clear springs.

Hongcun, also known as Hongcun (泓村), is twenty li south of Yixian county seat, located at the northern edge of the Yixian basin surrounded by mountains on all four sides.

It backs onto Leigang Hill at 360 meters above sea level (60+ meters relative height), covered with forests of sassafras; it faces the confluence of the Yongxi and Yangzhan rivers, tributaries of the Xin'an River — so-called "resting on a high ridge, facing flowing water, with an endless view" — its geographical location is very favorable.

It currently has 355 households and over 1,180 people, belonging to two production teams.

It is one of the famous ancient villages in Yixian with beautiful scenery, with eight scenic spots inside and outside the village such as "Leigang Dotting Green" and "South Lake Autumn Moon." At the village entrance by the stream there was originally Suiyang Pavilion, now destroyed; beside the old site stands a red poplar 18 meters high and 1.8 meters in diameter

[Three Development Stages of Hongcun's Water System planning]

The most stunning aspect of Hongcun's planning is its water system. Hongcun's water system was not built all at once but went through three development stages, gradually improving.

First stage: During the Southern Song Shaoxing period (1131-1162), Wang Yanji, the founding ancestor of Hongcun, moved from Qishu Village in Yixian due to war and built thirteen houses at the foot of Leigang Hill — the beginning of Hongcun. At this time, Hongcun was just a small village built against the mountain, with no systematic water system planning.

Second stage: During the Ming Yongle period (1403-1424), Wang Siqi (the 76th-generation ancestor of the Wang clan in Hongcun, who served as a grain transport registrar in Shanxi) invited fengshui master He Keda (from Xiuning) to "comprehensively survey mountains and rivers, carefully examine the veins" and formulate Hongcun's water system plan.

He Keda believed Hongcun's form was "ox-shaped" — Leigang Hill as the ox head, the two ancient trees at the village entrance as ox horns, the residences as the ox body, the Moon Marsh as the ox stomach, the water channels as ox intestines, and South Lake as the ox belly.

Thus, the Moon Marsh (a semicircular pond) was excavated in the village center as the "ox stomach," and over 400 meters of water channels (artificial canals) were dug to divert stream water into the village, flowing past every household, as the "ox intestines." This was the key stage of Hongcun's water system planning, laying the basic pattern of Hongcun as an "ox-shaped village."

Third stage: During the Ming Wanli period (around 1580), new development in Hongcun brought new contradictions to the living environment. Over 150 years had passed since the construction of the Moon Marsh and water channels, the population continued to multiply, buildings became denser, and the prominent contradiction remained water supply.

People worried that with the fierce sun from the southern mountains, even the "inner yang water" stored in the Moon Marsh within the village was insufficient

The Ming dynasty was an important period of Huizhou's second great development after the Southern Song.

At this time, capitalism was sprouting, and large numbers of Huizhou people skilled in business and getting rich bought land and built houses in their hometowns; on the other hand, Cheng-Zhu Neo-Confucianism also reached its peak, with clan power building large numbers of ancestral halls, and landlords and wealthy gentry also借助 patriarchal ideology and power to大规模 annex land.

This was a period with need and capacity for large-scale construction within a certain scope, and Hongcun's water system basically took final shape. This was the second stage of development.

By the Ming Wanli period (around 1580), new development in Hongcun brought new contradictions to the living environment. Over 150 years had passed since the construction of the Moon Marsh and water channels, the population continued to multiply, buildings became denser, and the prominent contradiction remained water supply.

People worried that with the fierce sun from the southern mountains, even the "inner yang water" stored in the Moon Marsh within the village was insufficient.

Thus Wang Kuiyuan and others "proposed diverting spring water from the western mountains, digging deeper and larger," excavating South Lake at the south of the village as the "ox belly." South Lake is bow-shaped, with an area of about 1.8 hectares, its banks built with bluestone, and a "painting bridge" in the lake dividing it into eastern and western parts.

The completion of South Lake further perfected Hongcun's water system, forming the complete ox-shaped pattern of "mountain as ox head, trees as horns, houses as body, bridges as feet, pond as stomach, channels as intestines, lake as belly."

[Enlightenment from Hongcun's planning]

Hongcun's planning is a product of history, certainly not without shortcomings, and should not be copied today. However, today when emphasizing the development and planning of villages and towns and putting rural construction on the agenda, Hongcun's pattern, water system, architecture, and environmental space obviously have reference value.

But the author believes what is more important is: in the planning and construction of socialist modernized new countryside, if we do not combine material civilization and spiritual civilization, if we do not pay attention to "inviting the efficacy and spirituality of mountains and rivers," but instead blindly push all landforms flat with bulldozers, build river channels straight, put up rows of identical barracks-style buildings, or ignore planning and let random construction happen — then we are not even as good as our ancestors from hundreds of years ago.

Hongcun's enlightenment to us, in this regard, is undoubtedly far more important than borrowing specific techniques.

Hongcun's planning embodies the wisdom of traditional Chinese village planning:

1. Conforming to and utilizing nature — Hongcun backs onto mountains and faces water, utilizing terrain and topography to introduce natural water systems into the village, forming a harmonious unity between humans and nature.

2. Combining practicality and aesthetics — the Moon Marsh and South Lake are both practical water sources for fire protection, drinking, and washing, and the landscape center of the village, with high aesthetic value.

3. Systematic planning and gradual improvement — Hongcun's water system was not built all at once but through the efforts of several generations, gradually improving, embodying systematic and sustainable planning.

4. Injecting cultural connotation — combining village form with the "ox-shaped" fengshui concept, endowing the village with cultural symbolic meaning and enhancing villagers' sense of identity and cohesion.

The combination is "achieving absolute, essential harmony between common sense and lively inspiration." (Quoted from Delacroix's Diary)

(The author received help from Zhou Haihua of the Yixian County Infrastructure Bureau and Wang Liang of the Hongcun Cultural Station during research and writing; hereby thanks them.)

本篇术语 Key Terms

宏村 / Hongcun Village (ox-shaped village) 牛形村落 / Ox-shaped Village (fengshui pattern) 月沼 / Moon Marsh (Yuezhao) 南湖 / South Lake (Nanhu) 水圳 / Water Channel (shuizhen) 汪思齐 / Wang Siqi (Hongcun planner) 何可达 / He Keda (fengshui master) 徽州民居 / Huizhou Dwellings 黟县 / Yixian County 雷岗山 / Leigang Hill 画桥 / Painting Bridge (Huqiao) 程朱理学 / Cheng-Zhu Neo-Confucianism

4. 徽州民居与传统建筑空间观——徽州民居笔记之三
中文

徽州民居与传统建筑空间观——徽州民居笔记之三

单德启

（原载于《建筑史论文集》第9辑，1988.6）

徽州民居不仅拥有优美的环境，典雅的建筑造型，而且有独特的室内外空间。徽州地区的老祖宗们不但讲究宅居的环境，还刻意经营、利用、美化宅居的内外空间。这从一个侧面体现着中国传统建筑的空间观，反映了历史上我国人民对建筑空间的自觉性。

建筑的实体、空间和环境，是对立统一、互为依存的。不同时代、不同地区和民族，由于社会生产方式的不同，以及自然条件、经济资源、生活习俗和生活水平的差异，存在着各自对建筑实体——空间——环境的不同理解、不同处置和不同的鉴赏情趣。把人类对建筑的认识简单地划分为实体、空间、环境三个历史阶段，比如认为只有格罗庇斯等一代大师推动了新建筑运动以后，人类的建筑观才迈入空间阶段，恐怕这种说法既不符合历史又不符合逻辑。

【中国传统空间观的精髓】

把建筑空间和大自然沟通汇合、融为一体，是中国传统空间观的精髓。

计成在《园冶》中说："轩楹高爽，窗户邻虚，纳千顷之汪洋，收四时之烂漫"就是形象地表述了建筑空间和无垠的大自然息息相通。苏东坡的《涵虚亭》有"惟有此亭无一物，坐观万景得天全"句；倪云林的《溪亭山色图》有张宣"江山无限景，都聚一亭中"题句，都表达了这种思想。

然而中国人不但在营建御苑、私园时受这种传统空间观的指导，即便在经营一座村墟、建造一幢民居时，这种传统空间观同样也在起作用。

徽州民居皆依山取势，傍水逶迤，村溪石径，深巷重门，粉墙花窗，亭台楼阁把山岚云雾、溪光树影导向了幢幢宅居之内。清代黟县人汪士通在《万竹山楼记》中对他的宅居就有如下的描述："余居山庄五里许，取道幽僻，其一由山径

【徽州民居的空间处理手法】

徽州民居的空间处理有以下几个显著特点：

1. 序列空间的营造。徽州民居的入口往往不是直接开门见山，而是通过一系列的空间过渡——大门、门道、小院、天井、厅堂——形成层层递进的序列空间。这种空间序列不仅具有功能上的意义（隐私、防御），更具有审美上的价值（节奏感、层次感）。

非常有趣的是，徽州民居的庭园、天井虽然都不大，但往往略加处理就形成了流动的序列空间。西递村黄炳文宅连排三幢民居前的狭长入口前院，以矮墙一分为三，门洞和花窗在两截矮墙上相互错开，使三个小前院既分隔又联系，空间在互相渗透着、流动着，既避免了狭长感，又显得精致多变、尺度宜人，非常亲切，其手法实在高明。

2. 天井的空间意义。天井是徽州民居的核心空间，它不仅是采光、通风、排水的功能性空间，更是连接人与自然的精神性空间。天井将天空、阳光、雨水引入室内，使封闭的住宅与自然保持联系。"四水归堂"的风水观念赋予天井以文化象征意义。

3. 室内外空间的渗透。徽州民居通过花窗、漏窗、门洞等元素，使室内外空间互相渗透。花窗不仅具有装饰功能，更是空间渗透的媒介——窗外的景色通过花窗进入室内，室内的生活通过花窗延伸到室外。

4. 虚实相生的空间美学。徽州民居的空间处理体现了中国传统美学中"虚实相生"的原则——实体的墙体与虚的天井、庭院、门洞形成对比，封闭的外墙与开敞的内院形成对比，暗的厅堂与亮的天井形成对比。

5. 小中见大的空间智慧。徽州民居用地紧张，空间有限，但通过巧妙的空间处理——借景、对景、框景、渗透——在有限的空间中创造出无限的意境。"咫尺山林"、"小中见大"是徽州民居空间处理的智慧所在。

【徽州民居空间观的启示】

徽州民居的空间观对当代建筑设计具有重要的启示意义：

1. 建筑空间不应是封闭的、孤立的，而应与自然环境沟通、融合。

2. 空间处理应注重序列感、层次感和节奏感。

3. 有限的空间可以通过巧妙的处理创造出无限的意境。

4. 空间不仅具有功能性，更具有精神性和文化性。

5. 传统空间观中的智慧值得当代建筑师学习和借鉴。

徽州民居的空间观是中国传统建筑空间观的典型代表，它体现了中国人对建筑空间的深刻理解和独特处理手法。在当代建筑设计中，我们应该继承和发扬这种空间观，创造出既符合现代生活需要、又具有中国文化特色的建筑空间。

English

Huizhou dwellings and Traditional Architectural space View — Notes on Huizhou dwellings III

By Shan Deqi

(Originally published in Architectural History Essays, Vol. 9, 1988.6)

Huizhou dwellings not only possess beautiful environments and elegant architectural forms, but also unique interior and exterior spaces. The ancestors of the Huizhou region not only paid attention to the living environment of residences, but also deliberately managed, utilized, and beautified the interior and exterior spaces of residences.

This reflects from one side the spatial view of traditional Chinese architecture, reflecting the historical consciousness of the Chinese people regarding architectural space.

The entity, space, and environment of architecture are opposites unified and interdependent.

Different eras, regions, and ethnic groups, due to differences in social production methods, as well as natural conditions, economic resources, living customs, and living standards, have different understandings, treatments, and aesthetic tastes regarding the architectural entity — space — environment.

Simply dividing human understanding of architecture into three historical stages of entity, space, and environment — for example, believing that only after masters like Gropius promoted the modern architecture movement did human architectural views enter the spatial stage —恐怕 this view neither conforms to history nor logic.

[The Essence of Traditional Chinese Spatial View]

Connecting and merging architectural space with nature is the essence of the traditional Chinese spatial view.

Ji Cheng stated in Yuan Ye (The Craft of Gardens): "With high and airy verandas and windows adjacent to emptiness, one can take in the vast expanse of a thousand qing and gather the splendor of the four seasons" — this vividly expresses that architectural space is closely connected with boundless nature.

Su Dongpo's Hanxu Pavilion has the line "Only this pavilion has no single object, sitting and observing all scenes captures the completeness of heaven"; Ni Yunlin's Stream Pavilion Mountain Color painting has an inscription by Zhang Xuan: "The infinite scenery of rivers and mountains, all gathered in one pavilion" — all express this idea.

However, Chinese people were guided by this traditional spatial view not only when building imperial gardens and private gardens, but also when managing a village or constructing a dwelling — this traditional spatial view was equally at work.

Huizhou dwellings all follow the mountain terrain, wind along the water, with village streams and stone paths, deep alleys and layered gates, powder walls and flower windows, pavilions and towers guiding mountain mist and clouds, stream light and tree shadows into every residence.

Qing dynasty Yixian native Wang Shitong described his residence in Notes on Wanzhu Mountain Tower as follows: "My residence is five li from the mountain village, taking a secluded path, one route by mountain trail

[Spatial Treatment Techniques of Huizhou dwellings]

The spatial treatment of Huizhou dwellings has the following notable characteristics:

1. Creation of sequential space. The entrance of a Huizhou dwelling is often not directly open to view, but through a series of spatial transitions — gate, doorway, small courtyard, patio, main hall — forming a progressively layered sequential space. This spatial sequence not only has functional significance (privacy, defense) but also aesthetic value (rhythm, layering).

Interestingly, although the courtyards and patios of Huizhou dwellings are not large, they often form flowing sequential space with slight treatment.

The narrow entrance front yards of three consecutive dwellings at Huang Bingwen's residence in Xidi Village are divided into three by low walls, with doorways and flower windows staggered on the two sections of low wall, making the three small front yards both separated and connected, with spaces permeating and flowing into each other — avoiding the feeling of narrowness while appearing exquisite, varied, and human-scaled, very intimate.

The technique is truly brilliant.

2. Spatial significance of the patio. The patio (tianjing) is the core space of Huizhou dwellings. It is not only a functional space for lighting, ventilation, and drainage, but also a spiritual space connecting humans and nature.

The patio introduces sky, sunlight, and rainwater into the interior, keeping the enclosed residence connected with nature. The fengshui concept of "four waters returning to the hall" endows the patio with cultural symbolic meaning.

3. Permeation of interior and exterior spaces. Huizhou dwellings use elements such as flower windows, lattice windows, and doorways to make interior and exterior spaces permeate each other.

Flower windows not only have decorative functions but are also media for spatial permeation — exterior scenery enters the interior through flower windows, and interior life extends to the exterior through flower windows.

4. Spatial aesthetics of virtual and real complementing each other.

The spatial treatment of Huizhou dwellings embodies the principle of "virtual and real complementing each other" in traditional Chinese aesthetics — the solid walls contrast with the virtual patios, courtyards, and doorways; the enclosed exterior walls contrast with the open inner courtyards; the dark halls contrast with the bright patios.

5. Spatial wisdom of seeing the large in the small. Huizhou dwellings have tight land use and limited space, but through ingenious spatial treatment — borrowed scenery, opposite scenery, framed scenery, permeation — they create infinite artistic conception within limited space. "A forest within a few feet" and "seeing the large in the small" are the wisdom of Huizhou dwelling spatial treatment.

[Enlightenment from Huizhou dwelling Spatial View]

The spatial view of Huizhou dwellings has important enlightenment significance for contemporary architectural design:

1. Architectural space should not be enclosed and isolated, but should communicate and merge with the natural environment.

2. Spatial treatment should emphasize sequence, layering, and rhythm.

3. Limited space can create infinite artistic conception through ingenious treatment.

4. space has not only functionality but also spirituality and cultural nature.

5. The wisdom in traditional spatial views is worth learning and borrowing by contemporary architects.

The spatial view of Huizhou dwellings is a typical representative of the traditional Chinese architectural spatial view, embodying the Chinese people's profound understanding and unique treatment of architectural space.

In contemporary architectural design, we should inherit and carry forward this spatial view, creating architectural spaces that meet modern living needs while possessing Chinese cultural characteristics.

本篇术语 Key Terms

传统建筑空间观 / Traditional Architectural Space View 序列空间 / Sequential Space 天井 / Patio (tianjing) 虚实相生 / Virtual and Real Complementing Each Other 小中见大 / Seeing the Large in the Small 借景对景框景 / Borrowed/Opposite/Framed Scenery 园冶 / The Craft of Gardens (Yuan Ye) 计成 / Ji Cheng (garden theorist) 花窗漏窗 / Flower Window / Lattice Window 四水归堂 / Four Waters Returning to Hall 西递村 / Xidi Village 室内外空间渗透 / Interior-Exterior Space Permeation
